K-12 Educational Outcomes in Nevada — Appendices

Long-form detail behind the two-page citizen brief. 165 bills found for 2019–2025; 100 policy bills carry the headline numbers.

Contents
  1. Appendix A — Every bill in the set — Every bill: plain topic, theme, result, where it ended, sponsors
  2. Appendix B — Theme scorecards — Theme scorecards with baskets and stop patterns
  3. Appendix C — Votes and support — Final Passage votes, party splits, high-support non-enactments
  4. Appendix D — Sponsors and people — Sponsors and people
  5. Appendix E — How far each bill got — Step-by-step progress for every policy bill
  6. Appendix F — What this data can and cannot say — What this data can and cannot say
  7. Appendix G — Did the text change on the way to the governor? — Introduced vs. final text for governor-bound bills
  8. Appendix H — Citizen statements, checked against the record — The ten most common citizen statements, each checked against the record
  9. Appendix I — Sources and review notes — Claim-to-source mapping, collection notes, review status

Committee Yea votes marked * are inferred (committee membership minus recorded Nay/Absent), because Nevada minutes usually list only No and Absent votes.

Appendix A — Every bill in the set

This record holds 724 bills found for 2019–2025: 513 policy bills (K-12 education policy is a real part of the bill) and 211 context bills (found by broad search terms or omnibus indexing; kept so the search is auditable). Headline numbers in the front brief use the policy set only. “Where it ended” tells you the last step the bill reached — the record never says why it stopped.

Appendix B — Theme scorecards

Each policy bill was assigned one theme. The basket says how this kind of idea usually fared in this record: Often moved before / Got support but didn’t finish / Rarely moved before / Mixed.

Pupil health, mental health, meals, discipline, and student and family supports

  • Bills: 97 · Became law: 51 (52.6%) · Basket: Mixed · Certainty: high
  • Typical stop: first committee (21), origin floor (14); vetoed (4)
  • Inference: support-staff and safety bills often become law, but school-adjacent health governance hit the veto desk in 2023 (82:AB201, 82:AB265).

Appendix F — What this data can and cannot say

Plain-language limits of the record behind this packet.

  1. The set was found by keyword search plus the official subject index; it is broad but not guaranteed complete.
  2. The record shows where each bill stopped - never why (no veto messages or floor debate in the dataset).
  3. Committee Yea votes are inferred (membership minus recorded Nay/Absent) because Nevada minutes usually list only No and Absent votes; these rows are marked in the source data.
  4. No OpenStates data in this collection; party labels come from official NELIS legislator directories (97.9% of roll-call ballot rows matched; the unmatched rows are minutes-parsing name fragments left unlabeled; sponsor party coverage is 99.6%).
  5. Concurrent resolutions carry no final NELIS history action, so their disposition reads Unknown (two adopted Financial Literacy Month resolutions) or In Progress (2025 AJR9); they are excluded from pass-rate claims.
  6. Special-session facts come from a manual verification file (verification/special-sessions.json), not the regular pipeline.
  7. Context bills (found by broad terms or omnibus indexing) are kept for audit but excluded from headline counts.

Known collection gaps recorded during the collection: 725 items (see sources/nevada/k-12_educational_outcomes/processed/data-gaps.json). OpenStates was unavailable for this refresh; all history, votes, and party labels come from the official NELIS system and legislator rosters, with committee votes read from official minutes PDFs.

Appendix H — Citizen statements, checked against the record

The ten most common citizen statements on K-12 educational outcomes from the Phase 2 Community Conversations (RAG constituent-voice dataset, “NV1 - Education” sheet), each checked against the 2019–2025 bill record. “Never filed” means no bill in this record proposed it — not that it is impossible.

1. Audit spending and direct more funding to the classroom

  • Status: Tried, but stalled
  • The record: The independent inspector-general design has been filed seven times in four sessions and never reached a floor vote: 80:AB146 and 80:AB296 (2019), 81:AB108 (2021), 82:AB149 (an Inspector General of Education) and 82:AB353 (under the State Controller) in 2023, 83:AB33 and 83:AB154 (2025). A Financial Oversight of School Spending Committee (82:AB395) also died in its first committee. What passed is legislative auditing: 82:AB517 (2023) requires the Legislative Auditor to performance-audit the two biggest districts and the charter authority at least every four years - it passed 42-0 and 21-0. Where similar ideas stopped: First committee, every time: Government Affairs killed the statewide inspector-general bills (2019, 2021, 2025); the Education committees killed the education-specific versions (2023). 83:SB411 (2025), which would have repealed the existing six-year outside-consultant reviews of district financial management, also died in committee - the review requirement survives in statute (NRS 387.607).
  • Where the decision sits: A state-legislature lever. Existing law already requires six-year financial-management reviews of districts by outside consultants and, since 2023, recurring legislative performance audits; the never-passed piece is an auditor independent of both the Legislature and the Education Department.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 AB146 Would have created a statewide, independent Nevada Office of the Inspector General to investigate state and local agencies Failed Died in its first committee
2019 AB296 Would have created a Nevada Office of the Inspector General inside the Department of Administration to investigate agencies Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2021 AB108 Would have created a statewide independent Office of the Inspector General Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB149 Would have created an independent Inspector General of Education to audit anyone receiving education money Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB353 Would have created an Inspector General under the Controller to audit school districts Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB395 Would have created the Financial Oversight of School Spending Committee to review district budgets quarterly Failed Died in its first committee
2023 SB344 Would have protected public comment at school board meetings, required audits, and set substitute licensing rules Failed Died in the second house
2025 AB33 Would have created an Inspector General under the Controller to audit anyone receiving public money Failed Died in its first committee
2025 AB154 Would have created an independent statewide Office of the Inspector General Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB411 Would have repealed the six-year consultant reviews of district financial management Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB517 Required recurring legislative performance audits of the two biggest districts and the charter authority Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - Why did the same design die in two different committees under sponsors from both parties? - Does the 2023 legislative-audit law (first audits due August 2026) answer the ‘outside entity’ demand or not? - Would an inspector general need new money, and does that route it through the money committees that killed 40 education bills?

2. Increase per-pupil funding / raise Nevada above the national average

  • Status: Precedent exists; specific designs still stall
  • The record: The machinery was rebuilt in this record: 80:SB543 (2019) created the State Education Fund and the Pupil-Centered Funding Plan (18-3, 34-7); 81:SB439 (2021) rebuilt its revenue streams (20-1, 36-5); 81:AB495 (2021) added a gold and silver mining excise tax routed to education (28-14, 16-5); 82:SB503 (2023) and 83:SB500 (2025) are the biennium funding acts (SB500 passed the Assembly 42-0 and the Senate 13-8). The 2023 session also passed 82:SB231, money for districts that budget raises for teachers and support staff. What keeps dying is the guarantee: 82:AB459 (2023), requiring the education budget to at least keep pace with revenue growth or inflation-plus-enrollment, died in Ways and Means; 83:SB471 (2025), changing how the Governor budgets the education baseline, died in Finance. Where similar ideas stopped: The money committees. Alternative revenue designs (county sales-tax options, 80:SB305; cannabis-tax rerouting, 83:AB307 and 83:AB508) died in Revenue, Ways and Means or on the floor.
  • Where the decision sits: A state-legislature lever exercised every two years in the biennium budget. No bill in this record sets a national-average target in statute; the Commission on School Funding is the standing body that computes and recommends the gap.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 SB543 Created the State Education Fund and the Pupil-Centered Funding Plan, replacing the Nevada Plan, the state’s previous funding formula Enacted Became law
2021 SB439 Overhauled State Education Fund revenues and the mechanics of the Pupil-Centered Funding Plan Enacted Became law
2021 AB495 New gold and silver mining excise tax with proceeds routed to the State Education Fund Enacted Became law
2023 SB503 The biennium K-12 funding act for 2023-2025 Enacted Became law
2023 SB231 Money for districts budgeting raises for teachers and support staff (the 2023 educator pay match) Enacted Became law
2025 SB500 The biennium K-12 funding act for 2025-2027: per-pupil base and weighted funding Enacted Became law
2023 AB459 Would have required the education budget to at least keep pace with revenue growth or inflation plus enrollment Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB471 Would have changed how the Governor budgets the State Education Fund baseline Failed Died in its first committee
2019 SB305 Would have let counties raise sales tax for pre-K and other programs and tapped marijuana tax for achievement Failed Died in its first committee
2025 AB307 Would have raised the retail cannabis tax with proceeds tied to the State Education Fund Failed Died on its first floor / calendar

Open questions for a working group: - What did the Commission on School Funding recommend as optimal funding, and how far short is the current biennium? - Would a statutory floor like 82:AB459 have survived a veto even if it had cleared Ways and Means? - Which revenue source fills the gap - the designs tried so far died in committee?

3. Mandate/protect recess and guaranteed instructional minutes (PE, art, social studies)

  • Status: Tried, but stalled
  • The record: The recess mandate reached the Legislature once: 83:AB53 (2025) would have required at least 20 minutes of daily outdoor recess in kindergarten through grade 5 and banned withholding recess for academic or behavioral reasons - exactly the enforcement gap participants described. It died in Assembly Education without a vote. 81:SB182 (2021), requiring PE to include activity scientifically shown to reduce obesity, died in Senate Education. 82:AB228 (2023), raising required high school social studies from three to four credits, cleared committee and died on the Assembly floor. Where similar ideas stopped: The first committee (Education), or the origin floor for the credit expansion. No minutes-guarantee bill has ever reached a floor vote.
  • Where the decision sits: Split venue. Statute already prescribes required subjects (NRS 389.018) and 180 school days, so a minutes mandate is a state lever - but day-to-day recess practice currently lives in district wellness policies, which is why provision varies school to school.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2025 AB53 Would have required at least 20 minutes of daily outdoor recess in elementary school and banned withholding it for academics or behavior Failed Died in its first committee
2021 SB182 Would have required PE to include activity scientifically shown to reduce obesity Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB228 Would have raised required high school social studies from three to four credits including geography Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2023 AB274 Required financial-literacy credits inside high school social studies and restructured the advisory council Enacted Became law
2025 SB444 Required school cellphone policies with progressive discipline and instructional-time limits Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - Why did AB53 never get a committee vote - opposition, or calendar? - Would a grades-limited mandate (K-5 only, as AB53 was drafted) answer the age trade-off participants raised? - Which districts already guarantee recess minutes by policy, making a statewide floor cheaper to enforce?

4. Adopt evidence-based, kid-centered classroom technology policies

  • Status: Never filed
  • The record: Nothing on the core design: no bill in this record requires independent proof of learning benefit before districts buy instructional technology, and no bill limits classroom screen time for young pupils. The nearest laws regulate the edges: 80:SB403 (2019) makes schools disclose which online services receive pupil data (40-0, 21-0); 81:SB66 (2021) tracks home Internet and device access; 83:SB444 (2025) requires district cellphone policies with instructional-time limits (42-0, 21-0); 83:AB406 (2025) bans artificial intelligence from doing school counselors’ mental-health work (42-0, 20-1). The state’s dedicated ed-tech body went the other way: 82:SB214 (2023) abolished the Commission on Educational Technology and folded its duties into the Department. Where similar ideas stopped: Nothing to stop - the proposal has not been filed. A 2025 transparency bill that would have required schools to post a public catalog of instructional materials including digital ones (83:SB248) died in Senate Education.
  • Where the decision sits: Mostly a district procurement decision today. The state lever exists - the Legislature has shown it will regulate school technology (privacy, cellphones, AI) - but purchasing standards would be new statutory ground.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 SB403 Required schools to disclose which online services get pupil data before pupils use classroom technology Enacted Became law
2021 SB66 Made the state track pupils’ home Internet and device access and work to close gaps Enacted Became law
2023 SB214 Abolished the STEM council and educational technology commission, moving duties to the education department Enacted Became law
2025 SB444 Required school cellphone policies with progressive discipline and instructional-time limits Enacted Became law
2025 AB406 Banned artificial intelligence from doing counselors’ mental-health work in schools Enacted Became law
2025 SB248 Would have required every school to post a public catalog of library and instructional materials Failed Died in its first committee

Open questions for a working group: - Should the standard bind the state’s own grant money first (ed-tech bought with categorical funds) before binding district general funds? - Who defines ‘independent research’ - the Department, or an outside body, given the Commission on Educational Technology no longer exists? - Does the cellphone-policy law (83:SB444) open a template for device-time rules for younger grades?

5. Universal pre-K and early childhood investment

  • Status: Precedent exists; specific designs still stall
  • The record: No bill in this record proposes pre-K for all children. The investment machinery exists and keeps growing: 80:SB84 (2019) put the state pre-K grant program in statute (41-0, 20-0); 82:AB400 (2023) added an Early Childhood Literacy and Readiness Account (41-0, 20-1); 82:AB348 (2023) and 83:AB212 (2025) built and extended a virtual family-engagement program for children entering kindergarten (38-4, 21-0 in 2025). The structural bills keep dying: a statutory Office of Early Childhood Systems passed committee and died on the Assembly floor in 2023 (82:AB113), and its Senate successor died in committee in 2025 (83:SB82); 83:SB58 (2025), which would have widened pre-K grant eligibility and tied grant size to per-pupil funding, died in Senate Finance; 83:AB292 (2025), requiring new elementary buildings to include pre-K space, died in Assembly Education. Where similar ideas stopped: Money and structure: Finance for the eligibility expansion, the Assembly floor and Government Affairs for the office bills. Program money routed as grants passes nearly unanimously.
  • Where the decision sits: A state lever exercised through the budget: pre-K seats grow through appropriations to the grant program, not through an entitlement statute. A universal guarantee would be new ground.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 SB84 Created the state grant program supporting prekindergarten programs statewide Enacted Became law
2023 AB400 Education omnibus: early-literacy account, school-funding commission duties, charter transportation money Enacted Became law
2023 AB348 Piloted virtual early-childhood family engagement before kindergarten, with money attached Enacted Became law
2025 AB212 Extended and renamed the virtual early-childhood family engagement program, with money Enacted Became law
2023 AB113 Would have created the Office of Early Childhood Systems in the Governor’s Office, with money attached Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 SB82 Would have created an Office of Early Childhood Services in the Governor’s Office Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB58 Would have widened who counts for state pre-K grants and tied grant size to per-pupil funding Failed Died in its first committee
2025 AB292 Would have required new elementary buildings to include designated pre-K space Failed Died in its first committee

Open questions for a working group: - How many children does the state grant program reach today, and what would universal coverage cost per biennium? - Why did the office-of-early-childhood design fail twice - turf, money, or redundancy with existing councils? - Does tying grant size to per-pupil funding (the failed 83:SB58 design) make pre-K funding automatic or just more visible?

6. Strengthen accountability for administrators and superintendents

  • Status: Precedent exists; specific designs still stall
  • The record: Principal accountability is now law twice over: 82:SB292 (2023) makes principals at-will employees when their school’s rating falls and half the teachers ask to leave (21-0, 39-3); 83:SB460 (2025) requires elementary achievement plans with consequences for principals and created a Public Education Oversight Board (21-0, 38-4). District-level accountability arrived the same way: 82:AB517’s recurring performance audits. Superintendent pay has never been touched: no bill in this record caps it, links it to outcomes, or changes principal pay-by-enrollment. School-board restructuring keeps failing: appointed-seat bills died in committee in 2019 (80:AB57, 80:SB105, 80:AB491), 2021 (81:AB255, 81:SB111) and 2023 (82:SB64, 82:SB65) before 82:AB175 (2023) added four appointed nonvoting members to the Clark County board (29-11, 16-4); the 2025 follow-up giving them full rights and term limits (83:AB156) passed the Assembly 24-18 and died in Senate Education. Where similar ideas stopped: Education committees, session after session, for board restructuring; the Senate for the 2025 appointed-trustee upgrade. The enacted accountability bills were carried from the Senate leadership side.
  • Where the decision sits: Split. Evaluation frameworks, ratings and audits are state levers and have moved. Superintendent contracts and principal pay schedules are district decisions made by elected boards under collective-bargaining law - the state record touches them only through the at-will law.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2023 SB292 Made principals at-will when their school’s rating falls and half the teachers seek transfers Enacted Became law
2025 SB460 Required elementary achievement plans with consequences for principals and created a Public Education Oversight Board Enacted Became law
2023 AB175 Added four appointed nonvoting members to the Clark County school board Enacted Became law
2025 AB156 Would have given Clark County’s appointed trustees full rights, set term limits, and curbed workshop votes Failed Died in the second house
2019 AB57 Would have made some Clark and Washoe school board seats appointed instead of elected Failed Died in its first committee
2021 SB111 Would have made some Clark and Washoe school board seats appointed instead of elected Failed Died in its first committee
2023 SB64 Would have added appointed members to county school boards and required district audits Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB517 Required recurring legislative performance audits of the two biggest districts and the charter authority Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - What have the first at-will principal removals under 82:SB292 looked like in practice? - Does the new Public Education Oversight Board (83:SB460) publish anything close to the ‘state of the school’ participants asked for? - Is superintendent compensation reachable by statute, or only through the elected boards that sign the contracts?

7. Increase teacher pay, support, and address the teacher shortage

  • Status: Precedent exists; specific designs still stall
  • The record: The raise happened: 82:SB231 (2023) sent state money to districts that budget salary increases for teachers and support staff (21-0, 41-1), and 83:AB398 (2025) funds extra pay for hard-to-fill positions (20-0, 41-1). The pipeline was rebuilt mostly unanimously: the Interstate Teacher Mobility Compact (82:SB442, 42-0), paid-pathway grants (82:AB515 and 82:AB428, both 42-0), student-teacher substitute rules (82:SB291), one-year provisional hiring of out-of-state teachers (83:AB49, 42-0 and 21-0), and recruiter-fee limits for visa teachers (83:AB472). What failed or died: the $45,000 minimum salary (80:SB445, R-sponsored, died in committee 2019), the 16-pupil grade-3 class-size cap with oversize pay (82:AB335), and the substitute-teacher insurance subsidy, which passed 31-11 and 16-4 and was vetoed (82:AB282). Bargaining expansions passed both houses and were vetoed twice (82:SB251; 83:AB155, the class-size-bargaining bill). Where similar ideas stopped: Salary floors and class-size caps die in the first committee; bargaining and benefit expansions die at the governor’s desk. Everything about supply - licensure, compacts, pathways - passes nearly unanimously.
  • Where the decision sits: State lever for money and licensure; actual salary schedules are district-union contracts. The 2023 state raise worked by conditioning new state money on districts budgeting raises - the tested template.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2023 SB231 Money for districts budgeting raises for teachers and support staff (the 2023 educator pay match) Enacted Became law
2025 AB398 Funded extra pay for hard-to-fill teaching positions and curbed weighted-funding diversions Enacted Became law
2019 SB445 Would have set a $45,000 minimum teacher salary and grants for relocating teachers Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB335 Would have cut the grade-3 class-size cap to 16 and paid teachers extra in oversized classes Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB282 Would have required a $450 monthly health-insurance subsidy for full-time substitute teachers Vetoed Vetoed by the governor
2025 AB155 Would have made class-size and support-staff ratios mandatory subjects of collective bargaining Vetoed Vetoed by the governor
2023 SB442 Enacted the Interstate Teacher Mobility Compact easing licenses for teachers moving to Nevada Enacted Became law
2023 AB515 Created the Incentivizing Pathways to Teaching grant program with tuition help and stipends Enacted Became law
2025 AB49 Let out-of-state licensed teachers start work provisionally for up to a year Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - Did the 2023 raise money reach paychecks in every district, and what happened where bargaining stalled? - Is the hard-to-fill differential (83:AB398) large enough to move anyone, and which subjects qualify? - Class-size reduction collides with the shortage participants named - what does the record say the Legislature chose when forced to pick?

8. Measure student growth rather than fixed proficiency; broaden assessment beyond core testing

  • Status: Tried, but stalled
  • The record: The purest ‘measure the delta’ bill died without a vote: 83:SB351 (2025) would have double-weighted the growth of the lowest-scoring pupils in school ratings; it died in Senate Education. The social-emotional measurement bill died on the Senate floor twice: 82:SB313 (2023) and 83:SB314 (2025), both aligning health standards with social-emotional learning standards and requiring teacher mental-health training, cleared committee and never got a floor vote. Alternative-assessment structures did somewhat better: competency-based education expansion passed the Senate 21-0 and died in the second house (83:SB403); the permanent competency-based approval process died in committee (83:AB24). What passed trims testing rather than redesigning it: 81:SB353 capped time spent on tests (42-0, 21-0), 82:SB9 ended end-of-course finals, and 83:AB401 - ending the college-and-career-readiness assessment as a diploma requirement - died in committee. Where similar ideas stopped: Senate Education for the growth-weighting bill; the Senate floor (twice) for social-emotional measurement; the second house for competency-based expansion in 2025.
  • Where the decision sits: A state lever: the statewide accountability system and its star-rating formula are set by the State Board under statute, and Nevada’s ratings already include a growth component - the failed bills changed its weight, not its existence.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2025 SB351 Would have double-weighted growth of the lowest-scoring pupils in school ratings Failed Died in its first committee
2023 SB313 Would have aligned health-class standards with social-emotional learning and required educator mental-health training Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 SB314 Would have aligned health standards with social-emotional learning and required teacher mental-health training Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 SB403 Would have created a metrics subcommittee, expanded competency-based learning, and allowed alternative schedules Failed Died in the second house
2021 SB353 Capped time spent on tests and required review of redundant assessments Enacted Became law
2023 SB9 Education cleanup: later expenditure-report deadlines, more CTE leadership money, ended end-of-course finals Enacted Became law
2025 AB401 Would have ended the college and career readiness assessment as a diploma requirement Failed Died in its first committee
2025 AB24 Would have turned the competency-based education pilot into a permanent approval process Failed Died in its first committee

Open questions for a working group: - How much of a school’s star rating currently comes from growth versus proficiency, and how far short of ‘measure the delta’ is that? - Why did the Lange bills die on the Senate floor after clearing committee twice - cost of the training mandate, or the standards themselves? - Does the metrics subcommittee created in the failed 83:SB403 design exist anywhere else in statute now?

9. Reform read-by-grade-three: reinstate retention, drop online assessment

  • Status: Tried, but stalled
  • The record: The record confirms the constituents’ history and shows no bill going their direction since. Retention was removed by 80:AB289 (2019), which replaced automatic third-grade retention with mandatory intensive intervention (28-11, 17-4) - no bill since has proposed reinstating retention. No bill repeals the assessment requirement either; the closest was 83:AB386 (2025), letting Washoe County pilot an alternative literacy assessment: it passed the Assembly 40-0 and the Senate 21-0 as amended on the session’s final days, then died awaiting an Assembly concurrence vote when time ran out. What did pass narrows the machinery: 83:SB278 (2025) dropped local assessments from deficiency decisions, leaving the statewide assessment as the trigger (42-0, 21-0). The phonics fight surfaced once: 82:AB187 (2023), banning three-cueing reading instruction in K-2, died in Assembly Education. Money: the 31st (2020) Special Session’s AB3 struck the FY 2020-2021 $31.4 million Read by Grade 3 transfer during COVID cuts and made it a top restoration priority; 82:AB400 (2023) created the Early Childhood Literacy and Readiness Account. Where similar ideas stopped: The finish line for the alternative-assessment pilot (concurrence never happened); the first committee for the phonics ban and the 2021 literacy grant account (81:SB273, Senate Finance).
  • Where the decision sits: A state lever: Read by Grade 3 is statute (NRS 392.750-392.760), and both the retention removal and the assessment rules were changed by bill. The specific assessment instrument is chosen by the State Board under that statute.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 AB289 Ended automatic third-grade retention under Read by Grade 3, requiring intensive reading help instead Enacted Became law
2025 AB386 Would have let Washoe County pilot an alternative elementary literacy assessment Failed Passed both houses, still no law
2025 SB278 Improved licensure access and dropped local assessments from Read by Grade 3 deficiency decisions Enacted Became law
2023 AB187 Would have banned three-cueing reading instruction in kindergarten through grade 2 Failed Died in its first committee
2021 SB273 Would have funded a literacy grant account for elementary reading programs Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB400 Education omnibus: early-literacy account, school-funding commission duties, charter transportation money Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - Would the Washoe pilot (83:AB386) have answered the online-assessment complaint, and does its 40-0/21-0 record invite a refile? - Ten years of results: what do the statewide reading numbers actually show since 2015 - the factual dispute participants could not settle? - Is there any sponsor on record for reinstating retention, given the associations’ standing objection?

10. Add school-based mental health services and integrated student/family supports

  • Status: Precedent exists; specific designs still stall
  • The record: The staffing framework is law: 80:SB319 (2019) defined school counselor, psychologist and social-worker roles and requires a full-time counselor where money allows (41-0, 21-0); 81:SB151 (2021) makes the two urban districts plan and report annually on improving support-staff ratios (33-8, 18-3); 83:SB277 (2025) does the same for social workers and eases their licensure pipeline (38-1, 15-5). Crisis infrastructure passed too: SafeVoice and Handle with Care (80:SB80), suicide-prevention policies (80:SB204), behavioral-health excused absences and crisis lines on pupil IDs (81:SB249), and a ban on AI doing counselors’ mental-health work (83:AB406, 42-0). The mandates keep failing: making the full-time counselor unconditional (83:AB298) died in committee in 2025, monthly mental-health workshops (83:AB374) died in committee, and the well-being screening policy (83:SB254) died on the Senate floor. The governance layer hit the veto desk: the statewide children’s mental health consortium (82:AB265) passed 42-0 and 20-0 and was vetoed in 2023, as was the behavioral-health oversight bill (82:AB201). Where similar ideas stopped: First committee for the unconditional mandates (they carry price tags); the governor’s desk for children’s mental-health governance in 2023.
  • Where the decision sits: A state lever for frameworks and money; actual counselor hiring is district staffing constrained by the same shortage as teachers - which is why every enacted law says ‘to the extent money is available’ and the bill that removed those words died.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 SB319 Defined school counselor, psychologist, and social worker duties and pushed full-time counselors where money allows Enacted Became law
2021 SB151 Required the urban districts to plan and report on improving counselor and support-staff ratios Enacted Became law
2025 SB277 Required school social workers where money allows and eased their licensure pipeline Enacted Became law
2019 SB204 Required suicide-prevention policies for every school and a model response plan Enacted Became law
2019 SB80 Renamed Safe-to-Tell as SafeVoice and created Handle with Care alerts when children face trauma Enacted Became law
2021 SB249 Excused school absences for behavioral health and put crisis lines on pupil ID cards Enacted Became law
2025 AB298 Would have made full-time school counselors mandatory instead of money-permitting Failed Died in its first committee
2025 AB374 Would have required monthly mental-health workshops for every grade Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB265 Would have created a statewide children’s mental health consortium; vetoed; health governance with school ties Vetoed Vetoed by the governor
2025 AB406 Banned artificial intelligence from doing counselors’ mental-health work in schools Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - What are the current counselor and social-worker ratios in the two urban districts under the 81:SB151 reports? - What would the unconditional counselor mandate (83:AB298) cost, and did a fiscal note kill it? - The 2023 vetoes hit governance, not services - does that leave a path through service money instead of new bodies?

Appendix I — Sources and review notes

This appendix holds the reviewer-facing material that does not belong in the front brief: the claim-to-source mapping, data-collection notes, and review status.

How this record was assembled

Bills were discovered two ways for the 2019, 2021, 2023, and 2025 sessions: NELIS full-text search (pupil, teacher, public schools, school district, charter school, per pupil, pupil-centered funding, State Education Fund, inspector general, recess, physical education, instructional time, educational technology, kindergarten, prekindergarten, early childhood, superintendent, class size, teacher salaries, achievement of pupils, college and career readiness, read by grade, literacy, school counselor, school social worker, school psychologist, school-based, and safe-to-tell — 438 bills) and a full harvest of the Legislative Counsel Bureau’s official Subject Index of Bills under the K-12 education heading family (EDUCATION, including the Department of Education, State Board of Education, Inspector General of Education, and education savings accounts; SCHOOL PUPILS; SCHOOL PERSONNEL; SCHOOL FINANCES AND FUNDS; SCHOOL ACCOUNTABILITY; SCHOOL FUNDING COMMISSION; SCHOOL DISTRICTS including the county district headings; SCHOOLS, PUBLIC / CHARTER / ACHIEVEMENT / EMPOWERMENT / FOR PROFOUNDLY GIFTED PUPILS; SUPERINTENDENT OF PUBLIC INSTRUCTION; TEACHERS bodies; TEACH NEVADA; EARLY CHILDHOOD EDUCATION; CAREER AND TECHNICAL EDUCATION; DISTANCE EDUCATION; ACADEMIC STANDARDS; LOCAL SCHOOL SUPPORT TAX; HOMESCHOOLING; SCHOOL CHOICE; SCHOOL PRECINCTS — +286 bills), with entry-level keywords (pupil, public school, school district, charter school, kindergarten, prekindergarten, early childhood, teacher, classroom, read by grade, literacy, recess, physical education, school counselor/social worker/psychologist, class size, per-pupil) harvested from all other headings. Higher-education-only headings (Nevada System of Higher Education, postsecondary institutions) were intentionally excluded: the issue is K-12 outcomes.

All six 2020–2025 special sessions were checked by hand and documented with full history and votes in sources/nevada/k-12_educational_outcomes/verification/special-sessions.json. Two K-12-relevant items exist, both from the 31st (2020) Special Session: AB2 (Clark County school-precinct fund-balance flexibility, placed on the Chief Clerk’s desk and never voted) and AB3 (the enacted COVID budget act, Chapter 5). AB3’s education provisions were verified from the enrolled bill text (leg.state.nv.us/Session/31st2020Special/Bills/AB/AB3_EN.pdf): section 71 struck the Fiscal Year 2020-2021 transfer of $31,429,229 for the Read by Grade 3 grant program, and section 131.6 made the New Nevada Education Funding Plan and Read by Grade Three accounts the top two priorities for restoration if federal offset money arrived. The 31st Session’s SB2 (Millennium Scholarship waivers, postsecondary) and the 36th (2025) Session’s AB5 (film-studio workforce act) were reviewed and excluded as outside the K-12 scope.

The search yielded 724 bills, hand-curated (every NELIS digest read) into 513 policy bills — 359 core plus 154 adjacent, the basis for all headline numbers — and 211 context bills kept for audit. This is broad, double-source coverage but not a provably complete universe; the record shows where each bill stopped, never why (no veto messages or floor debate). Committee Yea votes are partly inferred (committee membership minus recorded Nay/Absent) because Nevada minutes usually list only No and Absent votes; those rows are marked in the source data. Party labels come from official NELIS legislator rosters (97.9% of roll-call ballot rows matched; the unmatched rows are minutes-parsing name fragments left unlabeled; sponsor party coverage is 99.6%).

Generated provenance strings were corrected after the automated builds and are documented here: the discovery_note and data_limits fields inside evidence-pack.json (which the assembler stamps with wording from the first, water-scarcity run) were rewritten to describe the education headings actually harvested, and the issue-specific introduction sentences of Appendices A, F, H, and this packet’s README were corrected the same way. All counts are untouched pipeline output. Sponsor tallies merge NELIS name variants for the same person (Assemblywoman Selena Torres 2019–2023 + Assemblymember Selena Torres-Fossett 2025; Senator Carrie Buck + Senator Carrie Ann Buck; title changes from Assemblyman/Assemblywoman to Assemblymember in 2025); the merges are listed in reality-map.json. Concurrent resolutions carry no final NELIS history action, so two adopted Financial Literacy Month resolutions read Unknown and 2025 AJR9 reads In Progress; they are excluded from pass-rate claims. Every factual claim in the reality map and front brief was checked programmatically against the evidence pack before writing (working/nevada/k-12_educational_outcomes/fact-check-reality-map.py, all claims verified).

Statute and agency references

K-12 administration and accountability: NRS 385 and 385A (leg.state.nv.us/NRS/NRS-385.html, NRS-385A.html); school finance and the State Education Fund: NRS 387; the system of public instruction (courses, charter schools in 388A): NRS 388 and 388A; examinations and diplomas: NRS 389–390; educational personnel: NRS 391; pupils, attendance, discipline, and Read by Grade 3: NRS 392 (392.750–.760). Nevada Department of Education: doe.nv.gov; the Commission on School Funding publishes its recommendations through the Department; accountability data: nevadareportcard.nv.gov.
